7 science one-liners that can make any room go uncomfortably quiet
Story by Hannah Frey • 4mo
4. "The Universe Is Expanding Faster and Faster, Driven by Something We Named but Cannot Explain."
©Unsplash
Measurements in the 1990s showed that the rate at which the universe expands is accelerating, which was completely unexpected because nothing in known physics could produce this effect. Scientists gave this force a name, dark energy, but the name is essentially a placeholder. Dark energy is a term scientists use to refer to whatever is causing the universe to expand faster over time, and it's a catchall term because we don't know exactly what dark energy is, and no one has ever directly seen or measured it.

The two largest pieces of the universe, dark matter and dark energy, are the two that we know the least about, yet nothing less than the ultimate fate of the universe will be determined by them. Dark matter tends to pull the universe together, and dark energy tends to drive it apart, and a full understanding of this cosmic struggle will require major observational and theoretical breakthroughs. The universe is racing toward something, and the best minds on Earth can only watch.

7 science one-liners that can make any room go uncomfortably quiet
Story by Hannah Frey • 4mo
5. "Your Stomach Acid Can Dissolve Metal, and the Only Thing Stopping It from Eating You Alive Is a Thin Layer of Mucus."
©Unsplash
Stomach acid is strong enough to corrode steel, and the only reason it doesn't destroy us is a constantly renewing protective lining. Without it, digestion would become self-destruction. The lining of the human stomach replaces itself roughly every few days, making it one of the most rapidly regenerating tissues in the body.

This is not optional maintenance. It's continuous, urgent, and the moment it fails, the acid turns inward. This is easy to say in one breath at a dinner party, especially right before people start eating.

The body keeps dozens of similarly precarious arrangements running simultaneously, most of them invisible, all of them silently holding the whole system together. It's extraordinary when you think about it, and deeply unsettling when you think about it for a moment too long.

7 science one-liners that can make any room go uncomfortably quiet
Story by Hannah Frey • 4mo
6. "In Quantum Physics, It Has Been Proposed That Every Decision You Make Spawns an Entirely New Universe."
©Unsplash
Rather than having new universes spontaneously popping out of black holes, the many worlds theory proposes that every time we make a decision, a new universe is born. So basically every time you have to make a choice, whether it's paper or plastic, coffee or tea, debit or credit, you're essentially creating a new universe where you made the opposite choice. This is not a fringe idea.

It's a mainstream interpretation of quantum mechanics, debated seriously by working physicists. Each decision you make is then played out in full until it comes time for you to make another decision, at which point another universe would branch out. If this does in fact occur, that would mean there are a seemingly infinite number of universes out there for every decision every person has ever made.

Every trivial choice, every hesitation, every moment you almost did something differently, may have produced a complete and separate reality. Sit with that for a second.

Climate Compass

7 science one-liners that can make any room go uncomfortably quiet
Story by Hannah Frey • 4mo
7. "Statistically, It's More Likely That You Are a Spontaneously Assembled Brain with False Memories Than That the Universe Is Real."
©Pixabay
The Boltzmann brain thought experiment suggests that it is probably more likely for a brain to spontaneously form, complete with a memory of having existed in our universe, rather than for the entire universe to come about in the manner cosmologists think it actually did. This isn't a philosophical game. It arises from actual thermodynamic calculations applied to the long-term behavior of the universe.

Over a sufficiently long time, random fluctuations could cause particles to spontaneously form literally any structure of any degree of complexity, including a functioning human brain. The Boltzmann brain gained new relevance around 2002, when some cosmologists started to become concerned that, in many theories about the universe, human brains are vastly more likely to arise from random fluctuations, which leads to the conclusion that, statistically, humans are likely to be wrong about their memories of the past and in fact are Boltzmann brains. Most physicists believe there's an error somewhere in the reasoning that produces this conclusion.

The problem is, nobody has quite pinned down what that error is. What makes these one-liners so effective isn't that they're dramatic or sensational. It's that they're understated.

Said plainly, without buildup, each of these facts lands somewhere between wonder and dread. Science at its most honest doesn't always hand you comfort. Sometimes it just hands you a better question and a longer silence.

Popular Mechanics
703.1K Followers
Scientists Think They Know Where Consciousness Lives In Your Brain
Story by Elizabeth Rayne • 11mo • 3 min read


New research suggests that consciousness originates in the subcortex, and is polished by the cortex and cerebellum.
New research suggests that consciousness originates in the subcortex, and is polished by the cortex and cerebellum.
© VICTOR de SCHWANBERG/SCIENCE PHOTO LIBRARY - Getty Images
Here’s what you’ll learn when you read this story:

It used to be thought that without the cerebral cortex, consciousness could not exist.
New research, however, has found that humans and animals missing part or all of their cortices are still capable of having experiences associated with consciousness.
This research suggests that consciousness instead originates in the subcortex, and is polished by the cortex and cerebellum.
Whether consciousness is generated by quantum forces or connected to the entire universe is still up for debate, but even the theoretical physicists who suggest these complicated theories have not yet answered one question: Where are consciousness’ origins in the brain?

Ghostwriting & Publishing - Writers With 30+ Years Of Exp. - Team of Bestsellers
Ghostwriting & Publishing - Writers With 30+ Years Of Exp. - Team of Bestsellers
nexusbookwriting.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Peter Coppola, a neuroscientist from the University of Cambridge, sought to answer this question with an exhaustive analysis of brain studies going back decades. Consciousness is defined (at least in neuroscience) as “qualitative subjective experience, or ‘what it is like’ to be a specific organism in a specific state,” he said in a study recently published in the journal Neuroscience & Biobehavioral Reviews. Coppola wanted to see what the evidence said, rather than relying on the dominant theories.

While there are functions in all parts of the brain that contribute to consciousness, some might be more instrumental than others in giving us that awareness of who and what we are.

The prevailing thought is that consciousness arises from the outer-layer region of the brain called the cortex, which is mostly made up of the more-recently-evolved neocortex. The cerebellum at the base of the brain and the subcortex—which includes the brain stem and has not seen much change in 500,000 years of evolution—are thought to be involved in consciousness, but not so critical that they can power consciousness on their own. However, that might be about to change.

Related video: Scientists say consciousness isn’t what you think - here’s why (Sciencephile the AI)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Sciencephile the AI
Scientists say consciousness isn’t what you think - here’s why
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
0:00
0
Through his research, Coppola found studies showing that animals with part or all of their cortices removed—as well as humans who were born with conditions in which the cortex was partially missing—showed that they were still conscious to some extent. To gauge consciousness in a lab, the researchers used observable behaviors related to vision, movement, and verbalization. But those indicators bring with them a few questions. Is movement automatic, such as blinking, or does it have a purpose? Is vision following something, or just a startle response? Is verbalization intelligent or nonsensical? Even the absence of certain behaviors does not necessarily mean a subject is not conscious.

Besides the brutal and ethically questionable animal studies in which researchers surgically removed part or all of the cortex, studies of human children born with a rare condition known as hydraencephaly were telling. Hydraencephaly occurs when the cerebrum does not develop, and is instead replaced by cerebrospinal fluid. The only parts left are the basal ganglia (a cluster of nuclei in the subcortex associated with motor control, reward, and cognition), the meninges (membranes that line the skull), and the brainstem (which has been linked to consciousness in the past).

Online Courses For Adults - College For Your Schedule - University of Phoenix®
Online Courses For Adults - College For Your Schedule - University of Phoenix®
phoenix.edu
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Hydraencephaly often means a permanent vegetative state. However, studies of people born with the condition showed that their brains were capable of more than they appeared to be. In one case of a 32-year-old woman with a small part of her frontal cortex preserved, she was able to react to music with pleased facial expressions and vocalizations, even though the vocalizations were not understandable. This goes directly against the theory that consciousness cannot exist without the cerebral cortex. Observations from another study focusing on hydraencephalic children were similar.

Coppola’s research suggests that the base of consciousness lies in the subcortex, while the parts of the brain that evolved more recently—such as the neocortex—refine consciousness. However, this idea is highly nuanced, and further investigation is needed.

“If the embodied subcortex is truly sufficient for experience to emerge, then investigations may be undertaken to narrow down what specific subcortical functions engender experience,” he said. “[more research] may yield further insights as to minimal neural circuitry required for an experience.”

A Georgia widow's farm faced foreclosure. Then Trump stepped in to help
Story by Laerke Christensen • 4d • 6 min read


U.S. President Donald Trump stands at a podium and points to a photo of himself and Annabell Hill, a woman whose Georgia farm he helped save from foreclosure. IM WATSON/AFP, accessed via Getty Images
U.S. President Donald Trump stands at a podium and points to a photo of himself and Annabell Hill, a woman whose Georgia farm he helped save from foreclosure. IM WATSON/AFP, accessed via Getty Images

Claim:

U.S. President Donald Trump helped a farmer's widow clear her debts to keep her farm in Georgia after her husband died by suicide trying to stop it being auctioned off.

Rating:

True (About this rating?)

In September 2026, a claim (archived) circulated online that U.S. President Donald Trump helped a farmer's widow clear her debts to keep her farm in Georgia after her husband died by suicide.

A long text about the alleged act of kindness circulated on Facebook. It began:

On a February morning in 1986, a Georgia farmer named Leonard Hill walked out of his house twenty minutes before the bank was set to auction off his land. He never came back inside.

He took his own life that morning, believing his life insurance policy would cover the debts that had buried his family. It did not come close.

Leonard left behind a widow.

Annabel Hill was 66 years old, and the farm her husband died trying to save had been in her family for five generations.

Years of drought had wiped out the crops and the income that came with them. By the time Leonard died, the family owed the bank more than $300,000. His death did nothing to change that number.

The auction was still coming, and Annabel was now facing it alone.

That fall, an Atlanta businessman named Frank Argenbright heard what had happened to the Hills. He did not know the family. He simply decided that a widow in her sixties should not lose five generations of land because it stopped raining. So he started making phone calls, looking for anyone with the money and the will to step in.

Those calls eventually reached a New York developer whose name the Hill family had never heard. Donald Trump was 40 years old and building his reputation in Manhattan real estate.

Expand article logo  Continue reading


The same text and claim also circulated elsewhere on Facebook (archived), Instagram (archived) and X (archived). Snopes readers contacted us asking whether Trump really saved a Georgia farm.

Did TV chef Jamie Oliver win lawsuit against McDonald's over 'toxic' food claims?
Story by Laerke Christensen • 1w • 7 min read


TV Chef Jamie Oliver, a white man with brown hair wearing a white shirt and navy suit jacket, stands in front of a background showing two packs of McDonald's branded fries. James D. Morgan, accessed via Getty Images and Africa images, accessed via Canva, illustrated by Snopes
TV Chef Jamie Oliver, a white man with brown hair wearing a white shirt and navy suit jacket, stands in front of a background showing two packs of McDonald's branded fries. James D. Morgan, accessed via Getty Images and Africa images, accessed via Canva, illustrated by Snopes

In August 2026, a claim spread that McDonald's lost a lawsuit against the TV chef Jamie Oliver, who — according to posts — proved that the fast food chain's food was "unfit for human consumption" because it was "highly toxic." The claim is unproven. 
Snopes previously investigated a variation of this claim in 2018 and 2026. We found then and now that Oliver said in a 2011 TV program that beef trimmings used for a product he called "pink slime" were "not fit for human consumption." We found no evidence that he specifically called McDonald's food "unfit for human consumption" or "toxic," as the posts suggested.
The American Meat Institute, a trade association that represents processors of meat and poultry, said in 2012 that beef trimmings used to make "pink slime," which it calls Boneless Lean Beef Trimmings, were edible.
However, because we could not access a full version of the "Jamie Oliver's Food Revolution" episode where Oliver discussed "pink slime" to see if he specifically mentioned McDonald's, we leave this claim unrated.

Snopes will update this report if, or when, additional information surfaces. We contacted Oliver and McDonald's to ask whether Oliver has ever called the fast food chain's food "unfit for human consumption" or "toxic" or whether McDonald's ever lost a lawsuit involving Oliver and are waiting for responses.
A claim (archived) circulated on Facebook in August 2026 that fast food chain McDonald's lost a lawsuit against the TV chef Jamie Oliver after Oliver proved its food was "unfit for human consumption" because it was "highly toxic."

For example, one Facebook user wrote:

Expand article logo  Continue reading

McDonald's loses lawsuit against chef Jamie Oliver, who proved that the food they sell is unfit for human consumption because it is highly toxic. 

Chef Jamie Oliver won a lawsuit against the world's largest fast-food chain. Oliver demonstrates how hamburgers are made. 

According to Oliver, fatty cuts of meat are "washed" with ammonium hydroxide and then used to fill the hamburger patties. Even before this process, the TV presenter says, this meat was unfit for human consumption.

Oliver, a radical activist chef taking on the food industry, says: "We are talking about meat that is sold as dog food and then served to humans. Aside from the quality of the meat, ammonium hydroxide is harmful to health." Oliver calls it "the pink slime process." What sane person would put a piece of meat soaked in ammonium hydroxide into a child's mouth?

Perez Hilton death hoax spreads online after hospitalization
Story by Jack Izzo • 1mo • 2 min read


Perez Hilton, a younger middle-aged man with combed, middle parted hair and a mustache, wearing a black leather jacket. Denise Truscello, accessed via Getty Images
Perez Hilton, a younger middle-aged man with combed, middle parted hair and a mustache, wearing a black leather jacket. Denise Truscello, accessed via Getty Images

Claim:

In August 2026, the celebrity blogger Perez Hilton died from self-inflicted injuries he briefly livestreamed on TikTok.

Rating:

False (About this rating?)

On Aug. 4, 2026, the celebrity blogger Perez Hilton, born Mario Armando Lavandeira Jr., went live on the social media site TikTok for roughly 15 minutes. He was holding a knife and his torso appeared be covered in blood. Statements from Hilton's family later confirmed that his wounds were self-inflicted. Police responded to the home, according to People magazine, and Hilton was taken to a hospital. 

In the wake of the incident, posts spread online claimng Hilton had died from his injuries. 


An X post claiming that celebrity blogger Perez Hilton died on August 5, 2026.
An X post claiming that celebrity blogger Perez Hilton died on August 5, 2026.
(X user @PopBaseUpdates)

Snopes readers wrote in and searched the site looking for more information.

We found no evidence supporting the claim that Hilton had died because of his injuries, as of the time of publication. At least one account spreading the claim was a parody account of the celebrity news page PopBase, though we were unable to determine whether this was the origin of the rumor. Given the lack of evidence, we rate the claim false. 

Since the TikTok incident, Hilton's family has released three public statements on his website, two of which contained updated information about his medical condition. The first simply stated that he was receiving medical attention, while the second noted that his family still had little information about his condition but could confirm that Hilton was able to communicate with providers. All three statements thanked Hilton's fans for their support and prayers, while asking everyone to respect his privacy. 

Snopes contacted Hilton's team to ensure we would receive any additional updates about Hilton's condition.

If you or someone you know is experiencing a mental health, suicide or substance use crisis or emotional distress, reach out 24/7 to the 988 Suicide and Crisis Lifeline (formerly known as the National Suicide Prevention Lifeline) by dialing or texting 988 or using chat services at suicidepreventionlifeline.org to connect to a trained crisis counselor.

A mathematical rule against disagreement survives beyond quantum mechanics
Story by Rupendra Brahambhatt • 14h • 3 min read


A mathematical rule against disagreement survives beyond quantum mechanics
A mathematical rule against disagreement survives beyond quantum mechanics
Two people can examine the same evidence and reach different conclusions. However, if they share the same starting assumptions and know what the other believes, a theorem developed nearly five decades ago says their disagreement cannot survive under certain conditions. 

Now, two Chapman University researchers have shown that this principle works beyond classical probability, including in a framework that combines quantum theory with a classical model of knowledge and a wide range of generalized probability theories.

Online Courses For Adults
Online Courses For Adults
University of Phoenix
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
“We argue that agreeing to disagree is impossible in quantum theory. Secondly, and based on the quantum argument, we show that agreeing to disagree is also forbidden in any generalised probability theory,” the researchers note in their study.

This work suggests that the limits of disagreement may come less from the nature of reality and more from how information is updated.

The problem with taking agreement beyond classical physics
In 1976, economist Robert Aumann established the Agreement Theorem. It states that when rational agents begin with the same prior beliefs and their updated beliefs become common knowledge, they cannot continue assigning different probabilities to the same event.

Common knowledge means more than everyone knowing something. Each person knows it, knows that the others know it, and so on indefinitely.

The theorem originally relied on classical probability. This leads to an important question: Could quantum mechanics, with its unusual rules for describing physical systems, allow people to agree to disagree?

Related video: Knowledge: In quantum physics, more than one reality exists (Dailymotion)

Previous research explored extensions involving quantum scenarios and non-signaling systems. The authors of the current study took a different route, examining whether the theorem could survive when the mathematical description of probability changed. 

A mathematical rule that survives quantum theory
The researchers first developed a quantum version of the Agreement theorem using a classical, set-theoretical framework for representing knowledge alongside quantum mathematical objects called density operator-valued measures (DOVMs).

A DOVM allows researchers to describe information about events using quantum states rather than ordinary numerical probabilities. The researchers then defined a conditional quantum state, representing information available after restricting attention to a particular event.

This was crucial because Aumann’s original proof depends on conditioning—the process of updating a probability after acquiring information.

mathematical modeling with excel - Amazon Official Site
mathematical modeling with excel - Amazon Official Site
amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
They showed that when agents’ conditional quantum states become common knowledge, those states must be identical, provided the common-knowledge event has a nonzero quantum measure. 

However, this result applies to their particular hybrid framework, not every possible framework for representing quantum knowledge. The researchers then extended their reasoning to generalized probability theories (GPTs), which allow scientists to study probability systems beyond classical and quantum theory.

Their approach relies on two requirements. The first is a probability-like measure that adds consistently across mutually exclusive events. The second is a well-defined method for conditioning, or updating a state based on information. 

“In its probabilistic version, the Agreement Theorem is a direct consequence of how we choose to condition upon acquiring new information,” the study authors added.

Topology (2Nd Economy Edition)
Topology (2Nd Economy Edition)
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Using state-valued measures and conditional states, they showed that Aumann’s theorem applies to these systems when the necessary mathematical conditions are satisfied. 

What the result does, and does not establish
The study shows that Aumann’s Agreement Theorem is not limited to classical probability or quantum theory. Instead, its validity depends on the mathematical rules governing information updates. 

However, the researchers’ framework has limitations. It uses a static model of knowledge and cannot represent certain joint quantum states, including those involving non-Markovian processes and pre- and post-selection scenarios.

The researchers suggest that future work could examine communication between agents and broader versions of the theorem. 

For now, their findings establish that changing the rules of probability does not necessarily remove the mathematical conditions that prevent rational agents from agreeing to disagree.

The study is published in the journal Quantum.

2w ago
Study supports baryon junction model over three-quark view
What is challenged: STAR data indicate the textbook three-quark model oversimplifies proton and neutron structure, favoring a baryon junction description.

Key experimental clues: Scattering rates and differing movement of charge versus baryon number in heavy-ion collisions match baryon junction predictions.

Future research path: Researchers plan to test the model with other datasets and at the upcoming Electron-Ion Collider for more precise baryon probes.



Study supports baryon junction model over three-quark view
STAR results point beyond valence quark model
The STAR collaboration at Brookhaven’s Relativistic Heavy Ion Collider compared experimental data from gold, zirconium-96, and ruthenium-96 nuclear collisions with predictions from two competing models: the traditional valence quark model and the baryon junction model. In both scattering rate trends and the relative movement of baryon number versus electric charge, the data deviated from valence quark expectations but aligned with baryon junction predictions. These findings, reported alongside broader particle physics research, highlight how gluon field structures could play a central role in carrying baryon number. Physics World +1
Historical context of baryon number conservation
Baryon number conservation was articulated in 1938, before the quark model emerged in the 1960s. While the valence quark model assigns baryon number to quarks, theorists since the 1970s have explored alternatives. In 1996, Dmitri Kharzeev proposed associating baryon number with a Y-shaped gluon configuration—the baryon junction—reflecting the non-perturbative regime of quantum chromodynamics where quarks are deeply embedded in gluon fields. Morning Overview +1
Inside the quantum realm where reality turns into pure probability
Morning Overview
Quantum Physics For Beginners: The Simple Guide To Discovering How Theories Of Quantum Physics Can Change Your Everyday Life. The Secrets Of New
Amazon.com
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
Quantum Physics For Beginners: The Simple Guide To Discovering How Theories Of Quantum Physics Can Change Your Everyday Life. The Secrets Of New
Ad Choice
Why lattice QCD matters in this debate
At high energies, perturbative QCD treats quarks as isolated, making valence quark and baryon junction models effectively equivalent. In the non-perturbative lattice QCD regime, such as inside protons or quark–gluon plasma, the models predict different outcomes. STAR’s measurements exploit this regime, where gluon field dynamics become critical, offering an empirical window into non-perturbative QCD phenomena that complements other exotic hadron studies. Physics World +1
Next steps with the Electron-Ion Collider
The STAR team aims to analyze additional collision datasets and anticipates that the planned Electron-Ion Collider, set to replace RHIC, will serve as a more precise baryon probe. Such experiments could decisively test whether baryon number is fundamentally a property of quarks or of gluonic baryon junctions, deepening our grasp of hadron structure and strong-force dynamics. 6h ago
Hubble maps 30,000 newborn stars in giant superbubble
Massive stellar census: Hubble cataloged nearly 500,000 stars in and around N44, identifying about 30,000 as pre-main-sequence stars still forming.

Bubble’s violent origins: The 210×140 light-year cavity was created by stellar winds and supernovae, pushing gas into a dense shell where new stars emerge.

Early universe parallels: N44’s metal-poor environment mirrors early galaxy conditions, making it a valuable analog for studying ancient star formation.



Hubble maps 30,000 newborn stars in giant superbubble
Hubble captures 30,000 newborn stars in N44 superbubble
A new Hubble survey of the N44 nebula in the Large Magellanic Cloud has revealed nearly half a million stars, with about 30,000 identified as pre-main-sequence objects still in the process of forming. The superbubble’s vast 210-by-140-light-year cavity was hollowed out by intense stellar winds and supernovae from massive stars at its center. This activity compressed surrounding gas into a dense shell, creating ideal conditions for fresh star formation. wltreport.com +2
Why this stellar census matters for understanding star formation
The high-resolution Hubble data allows astronomers to map where stars of different ages are located relative to the superbubble’s structure. This spatial distribution helps test theories that the bubble’s expansion triggered new waves of star birth at its rim. Distinguishing true cluster members from foreground stars was essential to ensure the survey’s accuracy, which ground-based telescopes could not achieve at this detail. Astronomy +2
Ghostwriting & Publishing - Writers With 30+ Years Of Exp. - 100% Money Back Guarantee
nexusbookwriting.com
nexusbookwriting.com
·
Sponsored
Ghostwriting & Publishing - Writers With 30+ Years Of Exp. - 100% Money Back Guarantee
Ad Choice
From the Bubble Nebula to N44: linked by stellar wind shaping
Both the Bubble Nebula in Cassiopeia and the N44 superbubble in the Large Magellanic Cloud show how massive stars can reshape their surroundings through stellar winds and radiation. In the Bubble Nebula, a single hot, massive star drives a nearly spherical shell of gas against a molecular cloud, while in N44, a cluster of massive stars and supernovae has cleared a much larger cavity. These cases illustrate the recurring role of stellar feedback in sculpting gas and triggering subsequent star formation. nasa-apod-oss.vercel.app +2
What’s next for superbubble research
Astronomers plan to use N44’s visible cause-and-effect—massive stars alongside the shell they created—to refine timelines for triggered star formation. The Large Magellanic Cloud’s low metallicity offers a rare local proxy for early-universe conditions, enabling researchers to test models of how the first galaxies assembled. Future studies may combine data from Hubble with other space and ground-based observatories to track how energy and matter flow through such vast structures over time. wltreport.com +1

Climate Compass
16.8K Followers
The quiet experiment that changed modern science - and almost nobody heard about it
Story by Jeff Blaumberg • 2mo
The Experiment That Found Nothing - and Changed Everything
©Unsplash
In the late nineteenth century, Albert Michelson and Edward Morley designed an intricate experiment aimed at detecting the presence of "aether," a hypothesized medium through which light waves were thought to travel. The scientific community expected the experiment to confirm what everyone believed. It did the opposite.

Contrary to all expectations, Michelson and Morley observed no significant difference in the speed of light in any direction. This null result was initially met with skepticism, as it directly challenged the prevailing aether theory. The fact that there was no luminiferous aether turned out to be a huge advance for modern science, as it meant that light must have been inherently different from all other waves known at the time.

8 claims we've inspected about USS Abraham Lincoln
Story by Joey Esposito • 1mo
We've looked into reports of "dismal" conditions aboard the legendary aircraft carrier amid the Trump administration's combat operations in Iran.
© Nelvin C. Cepeda/The San Diego Union-Tribune via Getty Images
The USS Abraham Lincoln is an aircraft carrier for the United States Navy with a long, storied history. The vessel first launched in the late 1980s, and it soon became a crucial asset in operations such as Desert Storm, Enduring Freedom, Iraqi Freedom and more, including the recent conflict with Iran dubbed Operation Epic Fury. 

While the ship's legendary status was touted on official channels, reports circulated about grim conditions aboard the ship during its latest deployment, which began in November 2025 and was originally supposed to conclude in May 2026, according to the Navy Times. In July, the USS Abraham Lincoln set a new record for consecutive days at sea. 

However, in August, the BBC reported "food shortages, broken plumbing and exhaustion" and attempts by crew members to jump overboard. Concern was so great that Navy officials even met with the families of the deployed service members in a meeting rife with concerns for "the sailors' mental health, safety on deck, a lack of supplies, food shortages, water contamination and disruptions in the mail system," according to MS NOW. 

With the storied aircraft carrier front and center in headlines, we put together this collection of investigations regarding some of these recent claims — as well as a few far-older claims from the Snopes archives that involved some of the vessel's previous deployments.

Climate Compass
16.8K Followers
The quiet experiment that changed modern science - and almost nobody heard about it
Story by Jeff Blaumberg • 2mo
Why a Failed Measurement Became the Most Important Result in Physics
©Why a Failed Measurement Became the Most Important Result in Physics (This image is available from the United States Library of Congress's Prints and Photographs division under the digital ID cph.3b46036.This tag does not indicate the copyright status of the attached work. A normal copyright tag is still required. See Commons:Licensing., Public domain)
See more
The Michelson-Morley experiment was perhaps the most important negative experiment in the history of science. Its null result led directly to Einstein's Special Relativity. The chain of events that followed is staggering to consider, all because two scientists failed to find what they were looking for.

The resolution came eighteen years later, when Einstein's theory of special relativity arrived. With it came the recognition that the speed of light is a universal constant in all reference frames, that there is no absolute space or absolute time, and that light needs nothing more than space and time to travel through. It became a classic example of how empirical evidence can overturn long-standing theories and assumptions.

Climate Compass
16.8K Followers
The quiet experiment that changed modern science - and almost nobody heard about it
Story by Jeff Blaumberg • 2mo
The Quiet Dispute About Credit and Influence
©Pexels
One of the most debated topics in the history of special relativity has been the precise role played by the Michelson-Morley experiment in Einstein's creation of the theory. Einstein himself gave conflicting accounts over the years. The story of who influenced whom is more tangled than most textbooks suggest.

Historian Gerald Holton differentiates between Einstein's responses when asked about Michelson's influence and his spontaneous comments about the genesis of special relativity. When volunteering explanations, Einstein consistently emphasized the role of the Fizeau water tube experiment and stellar aberration rather than Michelson's work. Regardless of that complexity, Albert Michelson won the Nobel Prize in 1907 for his work developing the interferometer and the advances made because of his measurements.

Climate Compass
16.8K Followers
The quiet experiment that changed modern science - and almost nobody heard about it
Story by Jeff Blaumberg • 2mo
How Methodology Itself Became the Breakthrough
©Unsplash
The experiment's methodology, emphasizing precision and careful control of variables, continues to be a model for scientific investigation. This is a point that rarely gets enough attention. The instrument Michelson built was, in many ways, as consequential as the result it produced.

Precision tools redefine what questions science can even ask. A select group of experiments have stood the test of time in showcasing humanity at its most inquiring and intelligent. Whether elegant or crude, and often with a touch of serendipity, these singular efforts have delivered insights that changed our view of ourselves or the universe.

The pattern repeats across centuries: the real revolution is sometimes in the ruler, not the measurement.

Climate Compass
16.8K Followers
The quiet experiment that changed modern science - and almost nobody heard about it
Story by Jeff Blaumberg • 2mo
The Single-Cell Revolution Nobody Announced
©Unsplash
Closer to the present, a quieter kind of transformation has been taking place in molecular biology. Single-cell RNA sequencing technologies have established themselves as key tools for dissecting genetic sequences at the level of individual cells, revealing cellular diversity and allowing for the exploration of cell states and transformations with exceptional resolution. Most people outside the field have never heard of it.

Unlike bulk sequencing, which provides population-averaged data, single-cell RNA sequencing can detect cell subtypes or gene expression variations that would otherwise be completely overlooked. Researchers have developed bespoke tools for single-cell sequencing that have dramatically advanced a technology already transforming how scientists understand disease states, including scanning more than twenty-one million cells from every major mouse organ across five stages of life.

However, in August, the BBC reported "food shortages, broken plumbing and exhaustion" and attempts by crew members to jump overboard. Concern was so great that Navy officials even met with the families of the deployed service members in a meeting rife with concerns for "the sailors' mental health, safety on deck, a lack of supplies, food shortages, water contamination and disruptions in the mail system," according to MS NOW. 

With the storied aircraft carrier front and center in headlines, we put together this collection of investigations regarding some of these recent claims — as well as a few far-older claims from the Snopes archives that involved some of the vessel's previous deployments.

The quiet experiment that changed modern science - and almost nobody heard about it
Story by Jeff Blaumberg • 2mo
What Single-Cell Sequencing Revealed About Aging
©Pexels
These findings revealed that certain cell populations change in every organ, both in the same way and at the same time, during specific stages of life. This suggests that aging is not a linear process but instead a developmental stage sparked by specific molecular cues. That reframing alone has significant implications for medicine, pharmacology, and how we think about the biology of getting older.

Technologies such as single-cell RNA sequencing, spatial transcriptomics, and epigenomic profiling produce vast datasets that capture molecular states across millions of individual cells, revolutionizing the ability to investigate biological systems at the cellular level. The market for single-cell analysis technologies was estimated at over four billion US dollars in 2023 and is projected to grow rapidly through 2030.

The quiet experiment that changed modern science - and almost nobody heard about it
Story by Jeff Blaumberg • 2mo
AI Quietly Rewired the Engine of Discovery
©Unsplash
There is another transformation worth naming: the arrival of large language models and AI tools in active scientific research. The most visible example of accelerated discovery is the dramatic progress in protein structure prediction, culminating in the 2024 Nobel Prize. Problems that might once have consumed decades of experimental work can now be approached computationally, guiding laboratory effort and reducing the cost of exploration.

The field of antibiotics, long mired in stagnation, has been revitalized by deep-learning models able to screen millions of candidate molecules. Systems using such techniques have already identified compounds with entirely novel mechanisms of action, raising the real possibility of rebooting an area of research that had been considered commercially unviable and methodologically exhausted. It happened without a single press conference.

Climate Compass

The quiet experiment that changed modern science - and almost nobody heard about it
Story by Jeff Blaumberg • 2mo
Nature's Own Overlooked Experiments
©Rawpixel
Sometimes the experiment science missed for decades was hiding in plain sight in the natural world. The heart cockle has developed what appears to be the first known use of fiber optics in nature, using biological structures in its shell to channel sunlight to photosynthetic algae living inside. Researchers published this finding in 2024, and it drew modest coverage at best.

By mimicking that biological strategy, researchers built an ultra-sensitive microphone more compact than standard ones. Building on this invention, scientists said spider silk could continue to revolutionize sound technology, from helping identify hearing loss early to picking up low-frequency sounds known to precede tornadoes. A clam and a spider silk thread quietly handed engineers a new design philosophy.

Climate Compass
16.8K Followers
The quiet experiment that changed modern science - and almost nobody heard about it
Story by Jeff Blaumberg • 2mo
The Pattern Science Keeps Repeating
©Unsplash
Look across enough of these stories and a pattern emerges. The experiments that ultimately matter most are rarely the ones that confirm what we expected. They are the ones that produce a strange result, a null finding, a baffling anomaly that nobody quite knows what to do with.

Einstein's theory made a new reinterpretation of the Michelson-Morley experiment possible by associating it with a novel phenomenon, namely the invariance of the speed of light, a phenomenon that was not even the one originally investigated in the experiment. Scientific discoveries continue to demonstrate the importance of examining the world that surrounds us, from cosmic objects to the human body to prehistoric remains. The quiet experiments, the ones conducted without fanfare and published without ceremony, often turn out to be the ones that actually moved the needle.

The only thing missing was an audience paying attention at the right time.

Starlust
2.2K Followers
'Never encountered objects that act like this': NASA’s Chandra reveals a new class of cosmic objects
Story by Pranjal Nath • 1w • 3 min read


"Never encountered objects that act like this": NASA’s Chandra reveals a new class of cosmic objects
"Never encountered objects that act like this": NASA’s Chandra reveals a new class of cosmic objects
Scientists have found an entirely new class of cosmic sources in the data obtained by NASA's Chandra X-ray Observatory. These mysterious objects emit unusually low levels of X-rays while giving off intense ultraviolet (UV) radiation. "We’ve never encountered a group of objects that act like this," said Mustafa Muhibullah of the University of Alabama, who led the study, in a statement. 


A telescopic view of the Andromeda galaxy, which had 13 of the hypersoft X-ray sources. (Representative Image Source: Getty Images | Wirestock)
A telescopic view of the Andromeda galaxy, which had 13 of the hypersoft X-ray sources. (Representative Image Source: Getty Images | Wirestock)
In total, 84 of these objects, named "hypersoft X-ray sources" by the scientists, were found across six different targeted galaxies. These include our neighboring Andromeda galaxy (M31) and the Pinwheel galaxy (M101), both of which are spiral galaxies, and NGC 4472, NGC 3115, NGC 4697, and NGC 3379, all of which are ellipticals. As for what these sources might be, the likely explanation is that the energy is being produced by material being pulled from a star by a closely neighboring black hole, neutron star, or white dwarf. The star's matter is understood to be heating up in the process of falling into its companion and producing the unique type of radiation. While such binary sources have been observed before, none have been known to produce such low levels of X-rays and bright ultraviolet radiation.


This artist's concept depicts a supermassive black hole in the process of shredding a star to pieces (Representative Image Source: Caltech | R. Hurt)
This artist's concept depicts a supermassive black hole in the process of shredding a star to pieces (Representative Image Source: Caltech | R. Hurt)
"These clandestine X-ray sources are actually among the most energetic objects in galaxies, and they could be solving two cosmic mysteries at once," said Muhibullah. The first of the mysteries these hypersoft X-ray sources might resolve is how electrons are stripped from gases between stars. This stripping process is thought to be key to understanding the life cycles of galaxies but cannot be explained by the radiation from hot and massive stars alone. Since the hypersoft X-ray sources produce intense UV radiation, this discovery could go a long way in explaining the electron stripping process that continues to perplex astronomers. 


The remnants of a Type 1a supernova that erupted in 1604. A Type 1a supernova completely obliterates the white dwarf that causes it. (Representative Image Source: NASA/CXC/NCSU/DSS/M. Burkey et al)
The remnants of a Type 1a supernova that erupted in 1604. A Type 1a supernova completely obliterates the white dwarf that causes it. (Representative Image Source: NASA/CXC/NCSU/DSS/M. Burkey et al)
The second problem this discovery might help astronomers with is in finding which stars turn into Type Ia supernovae, which are a vital reference point for learning about the expansion of the universe. "Right now, we study them after they’ve exploded, and astronomers have struggled to understand what is actually ignited," noted Jimmy Irwin of the University of Alabama, who is also involved in authoring the research paper. Type Ia supernovae were what helped scientists establish that the universe is not just expanding but is doing so at an accelerating rate. This gave rise to the concept of the invisible force within our cosmos, which went on to be known as dark energy. "If we could find a way to spot these Type Ia supernova explosions before they go off, that would be really important," stated Irwin.


M101 with circles showing 7 of the newly discovered objects. (Image Source: X-ray: NASA/CXC/Univ. of Alabama/M. Muhibullah et al.; Optical: NASA/ESA/STScI; Image Processing: NASA/CXC/SAO/N. Wolk)
M101 with circles showing 7 of the newly discovered objects. (Image Source: X-ray: NASA/CXC/Univ. of Alabama/M. Muhibullah et al.; Optical: NASA/ESA/STScI; Image Processing: NASA/CXC/SAO/N. Wolk)
The discovery of these atypical sources of energy was not straightforward. This is because current X-ray telescopes cannot easily detect such low levels of X-ray emission. Moreover, hydrogen and helium in the interstellar medium readily absorb high-energy UV radiation, creating a barrier that is nearly impossible to look through.


An illustration of NASA's Chandra X-ray Telescope flying amongst stars. (Representative Image Source: NASA/CXC & J. Vaughan)
An illustration of NASA's Chandra X-ray Telescope flying amongst stars. (Representative Image Source: NASA/CXC & J. Vaughan)
The scientists found these sources among objects that appeared in the lowest X-ray-energy images in the Chandra archive but disappeared in higher-energy images. And since extreme UV lies next to the lowest-energy X-rays on the electromagnetic spectrum, the scientists were able to conclude that these sources would have to be emitting large quantities of highly energized UV radiation as well. “By combing through the Chandra archive, we were able to eliminate what used to be a blind spot for telescopes,” said co-author Rosanne Di Stefano of the Center for Astrophysics at Harvard & Smithsonian. “That’s how we found what appears to be a new class of cosmic objects with remarkable qualities.”

ReligionForBreakfast
3d3 days ago, 9/16/2026, 7:58 AM
Jesus wasn't actually called "Jesus" in Galilee - what his name probably sounded like
The video traces the name Jesus backward through English, Latin, Greek, Aramaic and Hebrew with historical linguist Dr. Benjamin Suchard. Jesus was a Galilean Jewish man living in a predominantly Aramaic-speaking region, and the transcript explains that his name was a form of the same name translated into English as Joshua. The later form Yeshua became common during the Second Temple period, although the video argues that its pronunciation in Jesus' lifetime likely lacked the prominent final "a" commonly heard today and ended with a guttural sound. Greek translators had no direct equivalents for several of those sounds, producing Iesous, which eventually traveled through Latin and later English to become Jesus.

Bizarre theories about the universe

Cosmic egg, the end of time: Bizarre theories about the universe
©Maurizio Degl Innocenti/EPA/Shutterstock

Philosophers, cosmologists and conspiracy theorists across the ages have sought to provide an answer, coming up with some truly mind-bending, sometimes misguided ideas about where we find ourselves and what happens next. Here are some of the many bizarre theories about the universe.

Bizarre theories about the universe
Story by Catherine Keogan • 1mo
Steady-state
©Shutterstock
The steady-state theory was conceived in the mid-20th century as an alternative to the Big Bang to explain the origin of matter and the expansion of the universe. Rather than the universe exploding outward, the theory posits that it has always expanded and will always expand, with new matter bubbling up all the time to keep it at the same density. The discovery in 1965 of the cosmic microwave background, leftover radiation from the Big Bang, ruled it out for good.

Cosmic egg
©NASA
Ancient Sanskrit texts describe a cyclical multiverse in which the universe emerges from a cosmic egg (Brahmanda), and expands outward from an infinite point (bindu) for billions of years before collapsing and being born again, foreshadowing many modern cosmological theories. The Hindu creation story grapples openly with the mysteries of: an unbounded universe, matter springing from the void, and the problem of the first cause: “In the beginning, there was neither nought nor aught…What then enshrouded all this universe? In the receptacle of what was it contained?... Only the Existent One breathed calmly, self-contained.”

The end of time
©Maurizio Degl Innocenti/EPA/Shutterstock
Time is a slippery beast in physics: is it a fundamental aspect of nature, a trick of perspective, a byproduct of thermodynamics or something else altogether? One new conjecture suggests time is not only real, it’s slowing down and will eventually stop altogether. In this view, the apparent acceleration of the universe’s expansion is an illusion created by a winding-down clock, rather than the effect of mysterious dark energy fields.

Heat death
©Cosmos/Fuzzy Door Prods/National Geographic/Six Point Harness/Kobal/Shutterstock
See more
One of the likelier fates of our expanding universe, the Big Freeze, hinges on whether it continues to expand. In this scenario, eventually all the stars burn out, black holes evaporate, the space between atoms grows infinitely vast and maximum entropy reigns: a cold, uniform soup of decayed particles where nothing ever happens. Fortunately, the heat death of the universe, as it’s also known, would take 10 to the power of 100 septillion years.

9 Medicare claims we've examined
Story by Rae Deng • 1mo
The federal health insurance program covers millions of Americans over the age of 65 and younger people with disabilities.
© designer491 from Getty Images
More than 66 million Americans are enrolled in Medicare, the U.S. government's health insurance plan for people 65 or older and some younger patients with disabilities. 

Given how complicated health insurance is — and how many people are affected by Medicare policy — rumors often spread about changes to the program. For example, Snopes has confirmed that President Donald Trump's administration announced the end of a Medicare subsidy program to help lower the cost of prescription drugs. We have also examined claims about supposed fraud in the program and allegations that various Republican politicians, including Trump, have sought to cut or end Medicare. 

Here are nine claims we've looked into about Medicare:

Geocentric model
©Michael Goh/Solent News/Shutterstock
As the stars rotated through the sky at night, it appeared to our ancestors that we were at the centre of the universe. Mankind’s centrality was so important that to suggest otherwise was, for a long time, heresy punishable by death. The planets of the solar system didn’t quite move in step with the distant stars, however—the word ‘planet’ is from the Greek for “wanderer”—requiring incredibly complicated models to explain their movements.

The Big Rip
©ESA/Herschel/PACS, SPIRE/Gould Belt survey Key Programme/Palmeirim et al. 2013
See more
What we don’t know about dark energy makes up more than two-thirds of the universe, but it appears to be why our universe’s expansion is accelerating. If this mysterious force pushing the galaxies apart gets steadily stronger, it could overcome gravity and eventually tear all the matter in the universe—maybe even space-time itself—apart. Fortunately, the Big Rip, as it’s called, wouldn’t happen for at least 80 billion years.

Computer simulation
©Warner Bros/Village Roadshow Pictures/Kobal/Shutterstock
See more
In 2002, Oxford philosopher Nick Bostrom hypothesized that if computers powerful enough to simulate consciousness can ever be developed, then at some point advanced civilizations will run these simulations—a lot. In fact, they would run these simulations so many times that it’s much more likely you live in one than not. (Yes, The Matrix.) The idea is so persuasive it’s convinced the likes of Elon Musk and astrophysicist Neil deGrasse Tyson, who puts the odds at “better than 50-50.”

Sciencephile the AI
3mo3 months ago, 6/9/2026, 5:58 AM
A single experiment ended 2,000 years of scientific belief
Science prides itself on self-correction — but history shows it has taken some spectacular wrong turns. For over two millennia, spontaneous generation was accepted as fact, claiming life could emerge from rotting meat or mud. Physicists defended invisible substances like phlogiston and luminiferous ether, while astronomers convinced themselves Mars was covered in artificial canals. These weren’t fringe ideas — they were mainstream science, until better experiments shattered them.

Sciencephile the AI
1mo1 month ago, 7/29/2026, 4:45 AM
A black hole weighing 100 billion suns was found - then scientists had to invent a new category
Astronomers may be looking at a black hole so extreme that the usual categories no longer seem big enough. The central black hole in Phoenix A is estimated at around 100 billion suns, placing it near the proposed class of “stupendously large black holes.” Its accretion disk could stretch more than 800 times the distance from the Sun to Pluto, and crossing its event horizon would not kill instantly — the fall inward could last about 18 days. The real mystery is whether objects this large grew like normal black holes or formed in the first moments after the Big Bang.

One solar flare could change life on Earth overnight
Space may look calm, but our own Sun is capable of unleashing disasters that directly threaten modern civilization. Powerful solar flares can disrupt satellites, cripple power grids, and shut down global communication in a matter of hours. Unlike rare cosmic catastrophes, these events happen regularly—and history shows they can come dangerously close to causing widespread damage. The real danger isn’t panic—it’s how unprepared we still are when the next major solar storm arrives.

The Brighterside of News
21.7K Followers
Inside the strange physics of why time stops at the speed of light
Story by Joseph Shavit • 1mo • 7 min read


Einstein’s relativity explains why fast travelers age more slowly and why time never passes at one universal rate. (CREDIT: Wikimedia / AI-Generated / CC BY-SA 4.0)
Einstein’s relativity explains why fast travelers age more slowly and why time never passes at one universal rate. (CREDIT: Wikimedia / AI-Generated / CC BY-SA 4.0)
© The Brighter Side of News
A clock aboard a fast-moving spacecraft does not break, lag or malfunction. It ticks normally for everyone inside. Hearts beat, coffee cools and hair grows at the usual pace.

Yet when the travelers return, they may have aged far less than people who stayed on Earth.

That unsettling result does not come from radiation, weightlessness or some hidden effect of space travel. It comes from the structure of time itself. According to special relativity, observers moving at different speeds can experience different amounts of time between the same events.

Online Courses For Adults - College For Your Schedule - University of Phoenix®
Online Courses For Adults - College For Your Schedule - University of Phoenix®
phoenix.edu
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
The idea sounds remote until you consider that nothing is ever truly still. Earth moves around the sun at roughly 30 kilometers per second. The sun circles the Milky Way at about 230 kilometers per second. The galaxy moves through the universe at hundreds of kilometers per second more.


Earth moves around the sun at roughly 30 kilometers per second. (CREDIT: Shutterstock)
Earth moves around the sun at roughly 30 kilometers per second. (CREDIT: Shutterstock)
© The Brighter Side of News
Those speeds do not provide a universal measure of motion. Physics offers no fixed background against which everything else can be judged. Motion only has meaning relative to another object.

No observer owns the correct viewpoint
Place two people in otherwise empty space and let them drift apart at half the speed of light. One person can say the other is moving. The second person can make the same claim in return.

Neither observer has privileged access to an absolute state of rest.

That principle once seemed like a technical detail. It became a crisis when physicists tried to understand light. Under ordinary experience, speeds add together. A ball thrown forward on a moving train travels faster relative to the ground than a ball thrown from a stationary platform.

Light refused to follow that rule.

Measurements taken in different directions and at different times of year returned the same result. Earth’s movement did not change the measured speed of light. The value remained 299,792,458 meters per second.


A ball thrown forward on a moving train travels faster relative to the ground than a ball thrown from a stationary platform. (CREDIT: Wikimedia / AI-Generated / CC BY-SA 4.0)
A ball thrown forward on a moving train travels faster relative to the ground than a ball thrown from a stationary platform. (CREDIT: Wikimedia / AI-Generated / CC BY-SA 4.0)
© The Brighter Side of News
That consistency forced a radical choice. If motion remained relative and light traveled at the same speed for every observer, then familiar ideas about distance and time could not remain fixed.

In 1905, Albert Einstein developed the framework that became special relativity while working in a Swiss patent office. His solution joined space and time into a single structure known as spacetime.

Related video: Here's why black holes aren't actually holes (XPLNR)
View on Watch
View on Watch

XPLNR
Here's why black holes aren't actually holes
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
0:51
0
Time could no longer serve as one universal clock for the entire universe.

Motion changes the passage of time
One way to picture relativity is to think of movement as divided between space and time. An object sitting still relative to an observer moves only through time from that observer’s viewpoint. Once the object begins moving through space, the amount of time recorded along its path changes.

The faster the spatial motion, the less time passes for the moving object compared with a stationary observer.

This effect is called time dilation.


In 1905, Albert Einstein developed the framework that became special relativity while working in a Swiss patent office. (CREDIT: Wikimedia / CC BY-SA 4.0)
In 1905, Albert Einstein developed the framework that became special relativity while working in a Swiss patent office. (CREDIT: Wikimedia / CC BY-SA 4.0)
© The Brighter Side of News
Nothing feels unusual to the traveler. Each second still feels like one second. Local clocks agree with nearby heartbeats, chemical reactions and everyday events. The difference appears when the traveler’s clock is compared with a clock that followed another path through spacetime.

The Concept Of Matter: A Journey From Antiquity To Quantum Physics (History Of Physics)
The Concept Of Matter: A Journey From Antiquity To Quantum Physics (History Of Physics)
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Consider a spacecraft heading toward a star 10 light-years away at 99 percent of light speed. From Earth, the trip lasts slightly more than 10 years. People watching from home see the ship cross the enormous distance.

The crew experiences about 1.4 years.

Their clocks do not suddenly tick in slow motion from their own perspective. Life aboard the spacecraft proceeds normally. The crew simply records fewer elapsed seconds between departure and arrival than observers on Earth.

At 99.9999 percent of light speed, the contrast becomes more dramatic. The travelers could experience the 10-light-year journey in roughly five days. A round trip could leave them only about two weeks older while approximately 20 years passed on Earth.

The effect is not presented as a trick of perception. Each observer measures a different elapsed time because each follows a different path through spacetime.

Distance does not remain untouched
Time dilation has a partner called length contraction.


The crew describes a stationary ship moving through a shortened stretch of space. (CREDIT: Wikimedia / AI-Generated / CC BY-SA 4.0)
The crew describes a stationary ship moving through a shortened stretch of space. (CREDIT: Wikimedia / AI-Generated / CC BY-SA 4.0)
© The Brighter Side of News
From Earth, the spacecraft crosses a distance of 10 light-years while its clocks run slowly. Inside the ship, however, the crew considers itself stationary. The travelers instead measure the distance between Earth and the destination as shorter.

Motion changes the measured length along the direction of travel.

That shortened distance helps explain why the journey can take only days for the crew. They are not crossing the same measured distance that observers on Earth assign to the trip. Their frame of reference contains a compressed route.

Both descriptions remain valid.

Earthbound observers describe a fast spacecraft with slow clocks. The crew describes a stationary ship moving through a shortened stretch of space. Relativity does not say that reality becomes arbitrary. It says that measurements of space and time depend on the observer’s motion.

The laws connecting those measurements remain consistent.

This distinction matters because relativity is often mistaken for the claim that every opinion is equally true. Einstein’s theory makes a much stricter argument. Different observers can produce different measurements, but those results must fit the same mathematical relationships.

The limit no massive traveler can reach
As an object approaches light speed, its time dilation grows stronger. The transcript describes this limit by asking what would happen if all motion through spacetime were directed through space.

At light speed, no proper time would pass along the path of light.

This leads to a tempting statement that a photon experiences no time between emission and absorption. A photon leaving a distant star and later reaching an eye does not age during its journey. Even if the trip spans billions of light-years from an observer’s viewpoint, the light itself accumulates no proper time.

The wording requires care because the transcript also qualifies the idea with the phrase “if it had” a perspective. The central point remains that light does not carry a clock that records elapsed time along its route.

Massive objects cannot join it.

Reaching light speed would require infinite energy for anything with mass. A spacecraft, person or particle with mass can approach the limit but never arrive there. Their clocks can run more slowly relative to other observers, but time never stops for them.

Timeless travel remains unavailable to people.

A universe without one shared present
The deeper disturbance comes from more than aging rates. Relativity also changes the meaning of “now.”

Observers moving at different velocities may disagree about whether two distant events happened at the same time. One can judge them simultaneous while another places one event before the other.

Neither observer needs to make an error.

Their disagreement comes from the relativity of simultaneity. The universe does not supply one master clock that divides existence into moments shared by everyone. Each moving object carries its own clock, and different paths produce different measurements.

At ordinary human speeds, these disagreements remain tiny. People on Earth move slowly compared with light and share similar motions. Daily life therefore creates the strong impression that time passes everywhere at one common rate.

That impression works well enough for conversation, schedules and most technology. It does not describe the full structure of the universe.

A commercial airplane travels at about 900 kilometers per hour. At that speed, the transcript places time dilation at only a few nanoseconds per hour. Human senses cannot detect such a difference.

Precision instruments can.

Atomic clocks flown on airplanes have recorded changes linked to motion. Particle accelerators also provide evidence because fast-moving particles persist differently than slower ones. The effect becomes part of practical engineering in satellite navigation.

Relativity enters everyday technology
Global Positioning System satellites require corrections connected to time dilation. Without those adjustments, the transcript states that navigation errors would grow by kilometers within hours.

A phone locating itself on a map therefore depends on clocks that do not all run at the same rate.

The effect also appears through gravity. A person’s feet sit slightly lower in Earth’s gravitational field than the head. According to the transcript, the feet age very slightly more slowly.

Such differences remain invisible in daily life. They matter when clocks become precise enough or when speeds become large enough.

Relativity is not confined to distant stars.

It operates during every flight, every satellite orbit and every high-speed particle experiment. Most of the time, the numbers are too small to notice. The rules do not switch off simply because human senses cannot detect them.

Time behaves less like a river
People often picture time as a river flowing past everything at one steady pace. Special relativity replaces that image with something less comfortable.

Time acts as a dimension tied to space.

Objects follow paths through spacetime, and the amount of time measured along those paths can differ. Speed changes the relationship. So does the observer chosen for comparison.

The transcript describes this relationship as a fixed spacetime “budget.” In that picture, a person moving slowly through space spends almost all of that budget moving through time. Light spends everything on motion through space and nothing on elapsed time.

That language offers an intuitive guide, though the underlying physics rests on the geometry of spacetime. The important point is not that motion steals a substance called time. Rather, space and time measurements adjust together so every observer obtains the same speed for light.

The universe protects that constant by giving up the idea of universal distance, universal duration and universal simultaneity.

Those sacrifices are larger than they first appear.

The original story “Inside the strange physics of why time stops at the speed of light” is published in The Brighter Side of News.

・The harsh physics behind interstellar travel and why Earth may never be visited by aliens

Did TV chef Jamie Oliver win lawsuit against McDonald's over 'toxic' food claims?
Story by Laerke Christensen • 1w • 7 min read


TV Chef Jamie Oliver, a white man with brown hair wearing a white shirt and navy suit jacket, stands in front of a background showing two packs of McDonald's branded fries. James D. Morgan, accessed via Getty Images and Africa images, accessed via Canva, illustrated by Snopes
TV Chef Jamie Oliver, a white man with brown hair wearing a white shirt and navy suit jacket, stands in front of a background showing two packs of McDonald's branded fries. James D. Morgan, accessed via Getty Images and Africa images, accessed via Canva, illustrated by Snopes

In August 2026, a claim spread that McDonald's lost a lawsuit against the TV chef Jamie Oliver, who — according to posts — proved that the fast food chain's food was "unfit for human consumption" because it was "highly toxic." The claim is unproven. 
Snopes previously investigated a variation of this claim in 2018 and 2026. We found then and now that Oliver said in a 2011 TV program that beef trimmings used for a product he called "pink slime" were "not fit for human consumption." We found no evidence that he specifically called McDonald's food "unfit for human consumption" or "toxic," as the posts suggested.
The American Meat Institute, a trade association that represents processors of meat and poultry, said in 2012 that beef trimmings used to make "pink slime," which it calls Boneless Lean Beef Trimmings, were edible.
However, because we could not access a full version of the "Jamie Oliver's Food Revolution" episode where Oliver discussed "pink slime" to see if he specifically mentioned McDonald's, we leave this claim unrated.

Snopes will update this report if, or when, additional information surfaces. We contacted Oliver and McDonald's to ask whether Oliver has ever called the fast food chain's food "unfit for human consumption" or "toxic" or whether McDonald's ever lost a lawsuit involving Oliver and are waiting for responses.
A claim (archived) circulated on Facebook in August 2026 that fast food chain McDonald's lost a lawsuit against the TV chef Jamie Oliver after Oliver proved its food was "unfit for human consumption" because it was "highly toxic."

For example, one Facebook user wrote:

Expand article logo  Continue reading

McDonald's loses lawsuit against chef Jamie Oliver, who proved that the food they sell is unfit for human consumption because it is highly toxic. 

Chef Jamie Oliver won a lawsuit against the world's largest fast-food chain. Oliver demonstrates how hamburgers are made. 

According to Oliver, fatty cuts of meat are "washed" with ammonium hydroxide and then used to fill the hamburger patties. Even before this process, the TV presenter says, this meat was unfit for human consumption.

Oliver, a radical activist chef taking on the food industry, says: "We are talking about meat that is sold as dog food and then served to humans. Aside from the quality of the meat, ammonium hydroxide is harmful to health." Oliver calls it "the pink slime process." What sane person would put a piece of meat soaked in ammonium hydroxide into a child's mouth?

The harsh physics that may forever prevent humanity from reaching another star system
Interstellar travel captures the imagination, but the laws of physics impose severe limits on what is possible. Einstein’s theory of relativity sets a cosmic speed limit that prevents anything from exceeding the speed of light, and approaching that limit would require unimaginable energy. Combined with lethal radiation and the enormous distances between stars, these barriers make traveling beyond our Solar System one of the most difficult challenges humanity could ever attempt.

America planned to drop 10-ton metal rods from space - taxpayers would have paid the bill
The US military explored placing 10-ton tungsten rods in orbit, capable of striking Earth at around Mach 10 with the force of a massive conventional bomb. The promise was irresistible: no explosives, no radioactive fallout and a target hit within minutes. ZME Science

Scientists found a hidden “golden rule” in abstract art — and your eyes may already know it
Story by Mihai Andrei • 4mo • 5 min read


Vassily Kandinsky – Composition 8 (1923). This type of art often seems confusing or even random to the average viewer.
Vassily Kandinsky – Composition 8 (1923). This type of art often seems confusing or even random to the average viewer.
© ZME Science
Abstract art has a bad reputation. For many people, it’s just shapes and lines, sometimes also some smears of paint thrown in seemingly randomly. Stand in front of it long enough and the same awkward question creeps in: am I missing something, or is there simply nothing there?

Grammarly's AI can tell you how to make your message more effective.
Grammarly's AI can tell you how to make your message more effective.
Grammarly
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
That suspicion has haunted abstract and modern art for more than a century. But now, a new study argues that abstract art carries a hidden mathematical structure. This structure doesn’t necessarily show up in the shapes, but rather in the way the “holes”, loops, and textures unfold across an image.

Real vs AI-made art
In the Wozownia Art Gallery in Toruń, Poland, visitors stood before two exhibitions of abstract images. One was made by the Polish artist Lidia Kot, who had created a set of black-centered works for a solo show. The other was made by a machine — a customized image-generating neural network, stripped of much of the order that normally guides its output. The researchers ensured that this AI-made pseudo-art matched the artist’s works in broad visual properties like brightness and color difference. Both sets were printed at the same size, using the same paper and technology, and shown in the same gallery space.

Related video: The science of visual afterimages: Why your brain lies to you (TheMagicMatt)
View on Watch
View on Watch

TheMagicMatt
The science of visual afterimages: Why your brain lies to you
Stare at the white dot in the middle of this
